# API Design and REST Best Practices

## Question 1: Your API Returns a List of Products. A Client Asks for Pagination. How Do You Implement It?

### Architecture & Implementation Strategy

When a client asks for pagination, the most common mistake is treating it as just adding `page` and `pageSize` parameters. In a production ASP.NET Core API, your pagination strategy directly determines whether your database performs an instant index seek or scans millions of rows, how much memory Kestrel buffers, and whether downstream clients and CDNs can cache responses.

In high-throughput systems, I break the pagination design down into three practical areas:
1. **Choosing the Right Algorithm:** Knowing when simple offset pagination works versus when you must use keyset (cursor-based) pagination.
2. **Defensive API Contracts:** Clamping page sizes, returning opaque continuation tokens, and avoiding expensive `COUNT(*)` queries.
3. **Optimized Database Execution:** Aligning EF Core queries with composite B-Tree indexes so queries remain constant-time ($O(1)$) regardless of page depth.

---

### 1. Architectural Strategy: Offset vs. Keyset (Cursor) Pagination

| Feature / Metric | Offset-Based Pagination | Keyset / Cursor-Based Pagination |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **HTTP Query Contract** | `?page=2&pageSize=20` | `?cursor=eyJpZCI6MTIwLCJjcmVhdGVkQXQiOiIyMDI2LTEwLTI0VDEyOjAwOjAwWiJ9&pageSize=20` |
| **Database Translation** | `OFFSET @skip ROWS FETCH NEXT @take ROWS ONLY` | `WHERE (CreatedAt < @c_date) OR (CreatedAt = @c_date AND Id < @c_id) ORDER BY CreatedAt DESC, Id DESC FETCH NEXT @take ROWS ONLY` |
| **Algorithmic Complexity** | **$O(N)$** — Database engine scans and discards $N$ rows. | **$O(1)$** — Constant-time index seek on B-Tree. |
| **Data Consistency** | **Vulnerable to Page Drift** (skipped/duplicated items on concurrent inserts/deletes). | **Stable / Resilient** across concurrent mutations. |
| **Random Page Navigation** | Supported (jump directly to page 15). | Not supported (sequential traversal only: Next/Previous). |
| **Primary Use Cases** | Internal Admin Portals, CRUD Back-Offices (< 50k rows). | High-throughput APIs, Mobile Feeds, Infinite Scroll, Large Catalogs (millions of rows). |

#### Why Offset Fails at Scale:
In SQL Server or PostgreSQL, executing `OFFSET 100000 ROWS FETCH NEXT 20 ROWS ONLY` forces the storage engine to walk the index, load all 100,020 rows into the buffer pool, discard the first 100,000, and return only 20. Under high concurrency, this exhausts database I/O and CPU. Keyset pagination solves this by converting the page boundary into an indexed range query (`WHERE` clause), enabling an immediate $O(\log N)$ B-Tree index seek followed by an $O(K)$ scan of only the requested $K$ records.

---

### 2. Keyset (Cursor) Pagination Implementation in ASP.NET Core & EF Core

#### A. Deterministic Ordering with Composite Tie-Breakers
Keyset pagination requires a deterministic order. If sorting by a non-unique column (such as `CreatedAt` or `Price`), an immutable unique tie-breaker (typically the primary key `Id`) must be appended.

```csharp
// Composite cursor record representing the pagination anchor
public sealed record ProductCursor(DateTime CreatedAt, long Id);
```

The database query must be backed by a matching composite index:
```sql
CREATE NONCLUSTERED INDEX IX_Products_CreatedAt_Id 
ON Products (CreatedAt DESC, Id DESC) 
INCLUDE (Name, Price, Sku);
```

#### B. Opaque Continuation Token Serialization
Exposing raw IDs or database timestamps in query parameters couples the client to internal persistence schemas and exposes predictable identifiers. Instead, encapsulate the state into an opaque, Base64Url-encoded token. For tamper-proofing, protect the token using ASP.NET Core Data Protection (`IDataProtector`).

```csharp
using System.Security.Cryptography;
using System.Text;
using System.Text.Json;
using Microsoft.AspNetCore.DataProtection;
using Microsoft.AspNetCore.WebUtilities;

public interface ICursorProtector
{
    string Encode<T>(T cursor);
    T? Decode<T>(string token);
}

public sealed class CursorProtector : ICursorProtector
{
    private readonly IDataProtector _protector;

    public CursorProtector(IDataProtectionProvider provider)
    {
        _protector = provider.CreateProtector("Api.Pagination.Cursor.v1");
    }

    public string Encode<T>(T cursor)
    {
        var json = JsonSerializer.Serialize(cursor);
        var protectedBytes = _protector.Protect(Encoding.UTF8.GetBytes(json));
        return WebEncoders.Base64UrlEncode(protectedBytes);
    }

    public T? Decode<T>(string token)
    {
        try
        {
            var protectedBytes = WebEncoders.Base64UrlDecode(token);
            var plainBytes = _protector.Unprotect(protectedBytes);
            return JsonSerializer.Deserialize<T>(Encoding.UTF8.GetString(plainBytes));
        }
        catch (Exception ex) when (ex is CryptographicException or FormatException or JsonException)
        {
            return default;
        }
    }
}
```

#### C. The $N + 1$ Read Pattern (Eliminating COUNT(*))
Avoid running an expensive `COUNT(*)` to check if a subsequent page exists. Instead, request `pageSize + 1` records. If $N + 1$ items return, `hasNextPage` is `true`. The extra record is dropped from the returned collection and used to generate the `nextCursor`.

```csharp
using Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore;

public sealed record GetProductsQuery(string? Cursor, int? PageSize);
public sealed record PagedResultDto<T>(IReadOnlyList<T> Items, string? NextCursor, bool HasNextPage, int PageSize);

public async Task<PagedResultDto<ProductResponseDto>> GetProductsAsync(
    GetProductsQuery query, 
    AppDbContext dbContext, 
    ICursorProtector cursorProtector,
    CancellationToken ct)
{
    // Guardrail: Enforce bounded page size server-side
    const int defaultPageSize = 20;
    const int maxPageSize = 100;
    var pageSize = Math.Clamp(query.PageSize ?? defaultPageSize, 1, maxPageSize);

    // Decode cursor
    ProductCursor? cursor = null;
    if (!string.IsNullOrWhiteSpace(query.Cursor))
    {
        cursor = cursorProtector.Decode<ProductCursor>(query.Cursor);
        if (cursor is null)
        {
            throw new BadHttpRequestException("Invalid or expired continuation cursor token.");
        }
    }

    // Build query with AsNoTracking() for read-only throughput
    IQueryable<Product> baseQuery = dbContext.Products.AsNoTracking();

    if (cursor is not null)
    {
        // Keyset predicate with tie-breaker
        baseQuery = baseQuery.Where(p => 
            p.CreatedAt < cursor.CreatedAt || 
            (p.CreatedAt == cursor.CreatedAt && p.Id < cursor.Id));
    }

    // Fetch pageSize + 1 to detect next page without COUNT(*)
    var rows = await baseQuery
        .OrderByDescending(p => p.CreatedAt)
        .ThenByDescending(p => p.Id)
        .Take(pageSize + 1)
        .Select(p => new ProductResponseDto(p.Id, p.Name, p.Price, p.CreatedAt))
        .ToListAsync(ct);

    var hasNextPage = rows.Count > pageSize;
    var resultItems = hasNextPage ? rows.Take(pageSize).ToList() : rows;

    string? nextCursor = null;
    if (hasNextPage)
    {
        var lastItem = resultItems[^1];
        nextCursor = cursorProtector.Encode(new ProductCursor(lastItem.CreatedAt, lastItem.Id));
    }

    return new PagedResultDto<ProductResponseDto>(resultItems, nextCursor, hasNextPage, pageSize);
}
```

---

### 3. API Governance & Web Standards

1. **Standard RFC 8288 Web Linking (`Link` Headers)**:
   In addition to returning pagination metadata in the JSON envelope, emit standard `Link` headers for RESTful hypermedia compliance:
   ```http
   HTTP/1.1 200 OK
   Content-Type: application/json; charset=utf-8
   Link: <https://api.example.com/api/v1/products?cursor=abc123token&pageSize=20>; rel="next"
   ```
2. **Defensive Bounds**:
   Always clamp `pageSize` (e.g., `Math.Clamp(requestedSize, 1, 100)`). An unconstrained client requesting `pageSize=500000` will saturate network buffers, trigger Gen 2 Garbage Collection pressure via Large Object Heap (LOH) allocations, and degrade the server process.
3. **HTTP Cache Optimization**:
   Keep cursor parameters strictly in the query string so intermediate CDNs (Azure Front Door, Cloudflare, Akamai) can key and cache immutable historical pages with ETags and `Cache-Control: public, max-age=300`.

---

### Notes & Terminology
- **API (Application Programming Interface)**: A formal computing contract defining interactions between software clients and server systems.
- **REST (Representational State Transfer)**: An architectural style for distributed hypermedia systems utilizing standard HTTP verbs, stateless requests, and uniform resource identifiers.
- **DTO (Data Transfer Object)**: An object designed exclusively to transfer structured state between service boundaries or across the network without exposing internal database entities or business logic.
- **EF Core (Entity Framework Core)**: Microsoft's official open-source, lightweight, cross-platform Object-Relational Mapper (ORM) for .NET.
- **LOH (Large Object Heap)**: A dedicated area of the .NET CLR memory heap for objects larger than 85,000 bytes. LOH collections require full Garbage Collection (Gen 2) and are prone to memory fragmentation.
- **B-Tree (Balanced Tree)**: A self-balancing search-tree data structure leveraged by relational database management systems (RDBMS) to perform logarithmic ($O(\log N)$) search, insert, and delete operations.
- **RFC (Request for Comments)**: Formal technical standards published by the Internet Engineering Task Force (IETF). RFC 8288 defines standard web linking.

## Question 2: What happens to offset pagination when a record is deleted between page requests?

### Answer

### Mechanics of the Problem: Page Drift (Phantom Shifts)

When an API utilizes offset-based pagination (`OFFSET K ROWS FETCH NEXT M ROWS`), the database evaluates pagination relative to an arbitrary numerical offset from the beginning of the sorted result set, rather than an anchored data point.

When records are deleted (or inserted) concurrently between consecutive page fetches, the entire result set shifts over the index. This phenomenon is known as **Page Drift** or **Phantom Shifts**.

#### Concrete Step-by-Step Scenario (Record Deletion):

1. **Initial State**:
   A table of 25 products sorted by `Id ASC` (Products 1 through 25).
2. **Request Page 1**:
   Client sends `GET /products?page=1&pageSize=5`.
   - SQL executed: `SELECT * FROM Products ORDER BY Id OFFSET 0 ROWS FETCH NEXT 5 ROWS ONLY;`
   - Client receives items: **[1, 2, 3, 4, 5]**.
3. **Concurrent Mutation**:
   While the client user is inspecting Page 1, a separate transaction or background worker deletes Product **3**.
   - The database now contains: **[1, 2, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, ...]**.
   - Note the physical shift: Product **6**, which was originally at index position 6 (the start of Page 2), has now shifted up into index position 5.
4. **Request Page 2**:
   Client sends `GET /products?page=2&pageSize=5`.
   - SQL executed: `SELECT * FROM Products ORDER BY Id OFFSET 5 ROWS FETCH NEXT 5 ROWS ONLY;`
   - The database skips the first 5 records: items `[1, 2, 4, 5, 6]`.
   - The database returns the next 5 records: **[7, 8, 9, 10, 11]**.
5. **Consequence**:
   **Product 6 is skipped entirely!** The user never sees Product 6, resulting in silent data omissions.

#### Reverse Scenario (Concurrent Insertion):
If an item is inserted on Page 1 while the user is reading it, all subsequent items shift downward by one slot. Product 5 is pushed to index position 6. When the user loads Page 2 (`OFFSET 5`), the database returns Product 5 a second time, resulting in **duplicate records** in the consumer UI.

---

### Architectural Solutions & Mitigations

#### 1. Keyset (Cursor) Pagination (Recommended Enterprise Standard)
Instead of relying on row numbers, anchor the query to the last visible item's unique key:
```sql
SELECT TOP (5) * 
FROM Products 
WHERE Id > @LastSeenId 
ORDER BY Id ASC;
```
If Product 3 is deleted, or Product 2.5 is inserted, the filter `WHERE Id > 5` evaluates deterministically. Products 6 through 10 are returned with zero skipped records and zero duplicates.

#### 2. SQL Server Snapshot Isolation / Repeatable Reads
If strict offset-based pagination is non-negotiable (e.g., legacy grid controls with arbitrary page jumping), configure the database transaction to use **Snapshot Isolation**:
```csharp
await using var transaction = await dbContext.Database
    .BeginTransactionAsync(IsolationLevel.Snapshot, cancellationToken);
```
Under Snapshot Isolation, the query reads from the SQL Server `tempdb` version store, presenting a transactionally consistent view of the table as it existed at the start of the transaction.
- *Architectural Trade-off*: Requires keeping long-lived client transactions open (stateful connection anti-pattern for REST APIs) or using session tokens tied to specific database transaction snapshots, which consumes high `tempdb` storage.

#### 3. Soft Deletes (`IsDeleted` Flag)
Rather than executing physical `DELETE` statements, apply soft deletes:
```csharp
public class Product
{
    public long Id { get; set; }
    public bool IsDeleted { get; set; }
    public DateTime? DeletedAtUtc { get; set; }
}
```
In EF Core, configure a Global Query Filter:
```csharp
modelBuilder.Entity<Product>().HasQueryFilter(p => !p.IsDeleted);
```
While this retains row offsets if configured properly, ongoing inserts still cause drift. Therefore, soft deletes only mitigate part of the problem. Keyset pagination remains the definitive architectural remedy.

---

### Notes & Terminology
- **Page Drift**: An anomaly in offset-based pagination where concurrent mutations (inserts/deletes) alter row index positions, causing duplicate or omitted records between sequential requests.
- **Snapshot Isolation**: A database transaction isolation level that provides a point-in-time view of data using row versioning in `tempdb` without blocking write transactions.
- **Soft Delete**: A database design pattern where records are flagged as inactive (`IsDeleted = true`) rather than physically removed from disk, allowing preservation of referential integrity and audit trails.

### Microsoft Documentation References
- [Microsoft Learn: Snapshot Isolation in SQL Server](https://learn.microsoft.com/dotnet/framework/data/adonet/sql/snapshot-isolation-in-sql-server)
- [Microsoft Learn: EF Core Global Query Filters](https://learn.microsoft.com/ef/core/modeling/query-filters)

## Question 3: If we cannot run a Count(*) query, how to get total pages or total count and implement pagination?

### Answer

### The Cost of `COUNT(*)`

Executing `SELECT COUNT(*) FROM LargeTable WHERE TenantId = @id` on tables containing tens of millions of rows forces the relational engine into a non-clustered or clustered index scan. Even if indexed, the database engine must count individual leaf-level index entries, resulting in high CPU usage, lock contention, and buffer pool eviction.

When solution architects design systems where running an exact `COUNT(*)` per request is prohibitive, four enterprise patterns are employed:

---

### Pattern 1: The $N + 1$ "Has More" Traversal Model (Modern Standard)

#### Architectural Concept:
Instead of presenting a static page count (e.g., "Page 1 of 5,420"), modern high-scale platforms (GitHub, Twitter, Stripe, Slack) adopt infinite scrolling or forward/backward navigation ("Next Page", "Previous Page").

#### Implementation:
Query `pageSize + 1` records:
```csharp
var items = await dbContext.Products
    .AsNoTracking()
    .Where(p => p.CategoryId == categoryId)
    .OrderBy(p => p.Id)
    .Take(pageSize + 1)
    .ToListAsync(ct);

bool hasNextPage = items.Count > pageSize;
var pagedData = hasNextPage ? items.Take(pageSize).ToList() : items;
```
- **Cost**: Zero additional queries. Exactly 1 roundtrip to the database.
- **UI Contract**:
  ```json
  {
    "items": [...],
    "pagination": {
      "pageSize": 20,
      "hasNextPage": true,
      "hasPreviousPage": false
    }
  }
  ```

---

### Pattern 2: Database Catalog Metadata & Fast Statistical Estimation ($O(1)$)

#### Architectural Concept:
When a business stakeholder demands a total count for general context (e.g., e-commerce search results: *"Over 1,200,000 products found"*), do not count rows dynamically. Retrieve the approximate row count from the database engine's statistical catalog tables in $O(1)$ constant time.

#### Implementation in SQL Server:
Query partition metadata via DMV (Dynamic Management View):
```sql
SELECT CAST(SUM(p.rows) AS BIGINT) AS ApproximateTotal
FROM sys.partitions p
WHERE p.object_id = OBJECT_ID('dbo.Products')
  AND p.index_id IN (0, 1); -- Heap (0) or Clustered Index (1)
```

#### Implementation in EF Core / Dapper:
```csharp
public async Task<long> GetApproximateProductCountAsync(SqlConnection conn, CancellationToken ct)
{
    const string sql = @"
        SELECT CAST(SUM(p.rows) AS BIGINT)
        FROM sys.partitions p
        WHERE p.object_id = OBJECT_ID('dbo.Products')
          AND p.index_id IN (0, 1);";

    await using var cmd = new SqlCommand(sql, conn);
    var result = await cmd.ExecuteScalarAsync(ct);
    return Convert.ToInt64(result);
}
```

#### PostgreSQL Alternative:
```sql
SELECT reltuples::BIGINT AS ApproximateTotal 
FROM pg_class 
WHERE relname = 'products';
```

- **Performance**: Sub-millisecond execution regardless of whether the table contains 1,000 or 500,000,000 records.
- **Accuracy**: Within 1-3% of the actual figure, updated automatically by background database statistics threads.

---

### Pattern 3: CQRS Asynchronous Read-Model Projections (Cached Counters)

#### Architectural Concept:
In a Command Query Responsibility Segregation (CQRS) architecture, the write side and read side are separated. When write operations occur (`ProductCreatedDomainEvent`, `ProductDeletedDomainEvent`), integration event handlers asynchronously update pre-aggregated count stores in Redis or a relational summary table.

#### Architecture Diagram:
```
[Client] ---> POST /products (Create) ---> [Command Handler] ---> Writes DB
                                                   │
                                            Publishes Domain Event
                                                   │
                                                   ▼
                                       [Counter Projection Worker]
                                                   │
                                          Atomic INCR/DECR
                                                   ▼
                                            [Redis Cache]
                                            Key: "counts:products:active"
                                                   ▲
[Client] <--- GET /products (Paged) <---- [Query Handler] Reads Fast Count
```

#### Implementation:
```csharp
// In-Memory atomic read in ASP.NET Core Query Handler via StackExchange.Redis
public async Task<long> GetTotalCountAsync(IDatabase redisDb)
{
    var count = await redisDb.StringGetAsync("counts:products:active");
    return (long)(count.IsNull ? 0 : count);
}

// Event handler incrementing on creation
public async Task HandleAsync(ProductCreatedEvent @event, IDatabase redisDb)
{
    await redisDb.StringIncrementAsync("counts:products:active");
}
```
- **Performance**: Sub-millisecond lookup from Redis RAM. Eliminates relational database load completely.
- **Consistency**: Eventual consistency (usually converged within milliseconds).

---

### Pattern 4: Single-Trip Window Function (`COUNT(*) OVER()`)

#### Architectural Concept:
If an exact count is mandatory for legacy contracts and the dataset is filtered by search criteria (meaning catalog statistics cannot be used), avoid executing two separate database network roundtrips (`COUNT(*)` followed by `SELECT ... OFFSET ...`). Combine both operations into a single query using SQL window functions.

#### Implementation:
```sql
SELECT Id, Name, Price, Sku, CreatedAt,
       COUNT(*) OVER() AS TotalFilteredCount
FROM Products
WHERE CategoryId = @CategoryId
ORDER BY Id ASC
OFFSET @Skip ROWS FETCH NEXT @Take ROWS ONLY;
```

#### EF Core LINQ Translation:
```csharp
var query = dbContext.Products
    .AsNoTracking()
    .Where(p => p.CategoryId == categoryId)
    .Select(p => new
    {
        Product = p,
        TotalCount = EF.Functions.Count()
    });
```
- **Trade-off**: Saves a roundtrip over the network, but the database engine still evaluates the predicate across matching rows. Best suited for small to medium filtered sets (< 100,000 rows).

---

### Notes & Terminology
- **CQRS (Command Query Responsibility Segregation)**: An architectural pattern that separates the data models and execution pipelines for update commands from read queries.
- **DMV (Dynamic Management View)**: Specialized internal SQL Server system views that expose real-time database state, storage engine structures, and partition health.
- **Window Function (`OVER()`)**: A SQL analytical function that performs calculations across a set of table rows that are related to the current row, without collapsing rows into a single grouping.
- **Eventual Consistency**: A consistency model used in distributed systems where, in the absence of new updates, all replicas or projections eventually converge to the same value.

### Microsoft Documentation References
- [Microsoft Learn: sys.partitions (Transact-SQL)](https://learn.microsoft.com/sql/relational-databases/system-catalog-views/sys-partitions-transact-sql)
- [Microsoft Learn: CQRS Pattern (Azure Architecture Center)](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/architecture/patterns/cqrs)
- [Microsoft Learn: Window Functions in Transact-SQL](https://learn.microsoft.com/sql/t-sql/queries/select-over-clause-transact-sql)

## Question 4: Your POST Endpoint Creates a Resource but Also Triggers an Async Background Job. What Status Code Do You Return?

### Architectural Assessment & Consistency Boundaries

Choosing between `201 Created` and `202 Accepted` isn't just a stylistic preference—it tells the client what promises the server is making right now versus what is being deferred.

Returning `200 OK` for a resource creation endpoint is misleading because it doesn't convey creation semantics or tell the client where to find the newly minted resource.

The real decision boils down to whether the resource itself is created synchronously before the response returns, or if the entire creation workflow is deferred:

---

### 1. Scenario A: Synchronous Resource Creation with Asynchronous Side Effects (`201 Created`)

#### Architectural Criteria:
- The core aggregate/entity (e.g., an `Order`, `Invoice`, or `UserProfile`) is validated, persisted, and transactionally committed to the primary database **during the HTTP request lifecycle**.
- The secondary action (e.g., sending an order confirmation email, generating PDF receipts, publishing analytics events) is an asynchronous side effect that does not block the primary entity's existence.

#### HTTP Semantics & Contract:
- **Status Code**: `201 Created`
- **Headers**:
  - `Location: https://api.example.com/api/v1/orders/ord-90421` (Points directly to the newly materialized resource).
- **Response Payload**: The created resource representation or summary DTO containing its permanent identifier and initial state.

#### The "Fire-and-Forget" Anti-Pattern in ASP.NET Core:
A critical flaw in naive implementations is using in-memory fire-and-forget constructs (e.g., `_ = Task.Run(...)` or unawaited async calls) inside a controller:
1. **Process Recycling & Data Loss**: When the host environment shuts down (IIS AppPool recycles, Kubernetes pod eviction, rolling deployments), running background threads are aborted without notice. Critical emails or billing jobs are lost forever.
2. **Captive Dependencies & `ObjectDisposedException`**: If the task captures scoped dependencies (such as EF Core `DbContext`), those services are disposed as soon as the HTTP request terminates, causing `ObjectDisposedException` inside the background thread.
3. **Thread Pool Starvation**: Heavy background work consumes .NET CLR ThreadPool worker threads, starving Kestrel's request-processing capacity.

#### The Solution: The Transactional Outbox Pattern
To execute side effects reliably, implement the **Transactional Outbox Pattern** with EF Core:

```csharp
using Microsoft.AspNetCore.Mvc;
using Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore;

[ApiController]
[Route("api/v1/orders")]
public sealed class OrdersController : ControllerBase
{
    private readonly AppDbContext _dbContext;

    public OrdersController(AppDbContext dbContext)
    {
        _dbContext = dbContext;
    }

    [HttpPost]
    public async Task<IActionResult> CreateOrder(
        [FromBody] CreateOrderRequestDto request, 
        CancellationToken ct)
    {
        // 1. Begin atomic transaction
        await using var transaction = await _dbContext.Database.BeginTransactionAsync(ct);

        // 2. Materialize primary entity
        var order = new Order(Guid.NewGuid(), request.CustomerId, request.TotalAmount);
        _dbContext.Orders.Add(order);

        // 3. Atomically persist an Outbox message in the same database transaction
        var outboxMessage = new OutboxMessage
        {
            Id = Guid.NewGuid(),
            OccurredOnUtc = DateTime.UtcNow,
            Type = nameof(OrderCreatedIntegrationEvent),
            PayloadJson = System.Text.Json.JsonSerializer.Serialize(
                new OrderCreatedIntegrationEvent(order.Id, order.CustomerId, order.TotalAmount))
        };
        _dbContext.OutboxMessages.Add(outboxMessage);

        await _dbContext.SaveChangesAsync(ct);
        await transaction.CommitAsync(ct);

        // 4. Return 201 Created with Location header pointing to the created resource
        var responseDto = new OrderResponseDto(order.Id, order.CustomerId, order.TotalAmount, "Created");
        return CreatedAtAction(nameof(GetOrderById), new { id = order.Id }, responseDto);
    }

    [HttpGet("{id:guid}")]
    public async Task<IActionResult> GetOrderById(Guid id, CancellationToken ct)
    {
        var order = await _dbContext.Orders.AsNoTracking().FirstOrDefaultAsync(o => o.Id == id, ct);
        return order is not null ? Ok(order) : NotFound();
    }
}
```

A dedicated hosted worker (`BackgroundService` or MassTransit Outbox Publisher) polls or streams the `OutboxMessages` table and safely dispatches messages to an enterprise broker (Azure Service Bus, RabbitMQ) with at-least-once delivery guarantees.

---

### 2. Scenario B: Deferred / Asynchronous Resource Materialization (`202 Accepted`)

#### Architectural Criteria:
- The resource **cannot** be created immediately because its creation requires heavy CPU processing, distributed verification, human approval, or multi-step batch workflows (e.g., video transcoding, bulk CSV ingestion, AI model inferencing, loan underwriting).
- Holding the HTTP request open would risk client gateway timeouts (e.g., Azure Front Door / Cloudflare 100-second limits) and tie up Kestrel server threads.

#### HTTP Semantics & Contract (RFC 9110 Asynchronous Request Pattern):
- **Status Code**: `202 Accepted` ("The request has been accepted for processing, but processing is not complete").
- **Headers**:
  - `Location: https://api.example.com/api/v1/operations/job-7712` (Points to an **Operation Monitor** resource, **NOT** the target resource, since the target resource does not yet exist).
  - `Retry-After: 30` (Directs the client to wait 30 seconds before polling, mitigating thundering-herd issues).
- **Response Payload**: An operation status descriptor DTO:
  ```json
  {
    "operationId": "job-7712",
    "status": "Accepted",
    "createdAtUtc": "2026-10-24T14:00:00Z",
    "retryAfterSeconds": 30,
    "links": {
      "status": "https://api.example.com/api/v1/operations/job-7712"
    }
  }
  ```

---

### 3. Idempotency & Resiliency Across Both Scenarios

Because network disruptions often cause clients to retry POST requests, any POST endpoint that triggers background operations must enforce **Idempotency Keys** (`Idempotency-Key: <UUID>`):
1. **Pre-execution Check**: In ASP.NET Core middleware or endpoint filter, check Redis or a relational table for the `Idempotency-Key`.
2. **In-Flight Detection**: If an operation is already executing under that key, return `409 Conflict` or the existing `202 Accepted` status payload.
3. **Cached Terminal Response**: If the operation previously completed, immediately replay the cached `201 Created` or `200 OK` response without re-triggering duplicate background jobs.

---

### Notes & Terminology
- **HTTP (Hypertext Transfer Protocol)**: The foundational application-layer protocol for distributed hypermedia information systems.
- **RFC (Request for Comments)**: Technical specifications defined by the Internet Engineering Task Force (IETF). RFC 9110 defines HTTP Semantics.
- **Transactional Outbox Pattern**: A reliable distributed messaging pattern where domain entities and outbound integration messages are persisted together inside a single database transaction, ensuring zero message loss even if message brokers are temporarily unavailable.
- **Captive Dependency**: A dependency injection anti-pattern where a service with a longer lifetime (e.g., Singleton) holds a reference to a service with a shorter lifetime (e.g., Scoped), leading to memory leaks or concurrency conflicts.
- **Idempotency Key**: A unique client-generated token (often a UUID/GUID) sent in HTTP headers to guarantee that retrying a non-idempotent request (like POST) executes exactly once.

### Microsoft Documentation References
- [Microsoft Learn: Asynchronous Request-Reply Pattern (Azure Architecture Center)](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/architecture/patterns/async-request-reply)
- [Microsoft Learn: Transactional Outbox Pattern with EF Core](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/architecture/patterns/transactional-outbox)
- [Microsoft Learn: Background Tasks and Hosted Services in ASP.NET Core](https://learn.microsoft.com/aspnet/core/fundamentals/host/hosted-services)

## Question 5: What happens if the background job fails? How does the client know?

### Answer

### Asynchronous Failure Handling

When an asynchronous background job fails, the original HTTP request context is already terminated. The application architecture must provide robust mechanisms across two distinct dimensions:
1. **Client Notification Mechanisms** (How the client learns of the failure).
2. **Server-Side Fault Tolerance & State Compensation** (How the system recovers or isolates the failure).

---

### 1. Client Notification Paradigms

Depending on client architecture (Single Page Application, Mobile App, or Server-to-Server B2B Integration), solution architects employ three primary communication strategies:

#### A. The Pull Model (Operation Status Polling)
The client periodically queries the monitor URL provided in the initial `Location` header (`GET /api/v1/operations/{operationId}`).

- When the background job encounters an unrecoverable failure, the status endpoint returns `200 OK` containing an error envelope conforming to the **RFC 7807 / RFC 9457 `ProblemDetails`** specification.
- Returning `200 OK` for the polling request is correct because the polling query itself succeeded in finding the operation state:

```json
{
  "operationId": "job-7712",
  "status": "Failed",
  "completedAtUtc": "2026-10-24T14:05:12Z",
  "error": {
    "type": "https://api.example.com/errors/video-transcode-unsupported-codec",
    "title": "Video Transcoding Failed",
    "status": 422,
    "detail": "The uploaded video contains an audio stream encoded in DTS, which is unsupported for web output profiles.",
    "instance": "/api/v1/operations/job-7712"
  }
}
```

#### B. The Push Model (Real-Time WebSockets via ASP.NET Core SignalR)
For interactive Single Page Applications (React/Angular/Blazor) or mobile apps, polling creates unnecessary network and database overhead. 

1. The client establishes a persistent WebSocket connection to an ASP.NET Core SignalR hub:
   ```csharp
   await connection.start();
   await connection.invoke("SubscribeToOperation", operationId);
   ```
2. When the background consumer (e.g., MassTransit or worker service) finishes or fails, it publishes a notification across a Redis backplane to the SignalR hub.
3. The hub invokes a client-side callback:
   ```csharp
   await _hubContext.Clients.Group(operationId.ToString())
       .SendAsync("OnOperationFailed", new { OperationId = operationId, Error = failureMessage }, ct);
   ```

#### C. Asynchronous Webhooks (B2B Integrations)
For server-to-server systems where clients cannot maintain open sockets, the client registers a `callbackUrl` with the initial POST request.
- Upon job failure, the worker enqueues a `DeliverWebhookNotificationCommand`.
- A resilient webhook delivery service sends an authenticated `POST` request to the client's endpoint with an HMAC-SHA256 signature (`X-Hub-Signature-256`) for payload verification, backed by exponential retry policies.

---

### 2. Server-Side Fault Tolerance & Recovery Patterns

#### A. Retry Policies with Exponential Backoff & Jitter
Transient failures (e.g., SQL deadlocks, network timeouts, downstream rate limits) should be retried automatically before declaring the job failed.
- In .NET, use **Polly v8** or broker-native retries (MassTransit / Azure Service Bus):
  $$\text{Delay} = \min(\text{MaxDelay}, \text{InitialDelay} \times 2^{\text{attempt}}) \pm \text{Jitter}$$
- Adding random jitter prevents the "thundering herd" problem where multiple retrying workers assault a recovering database at the same millisecond.

#### B. Dead Letter Queue (DLQ)
When all retry attempts are exhausted, non-transient failures (poison messages) must not be silently dropped. The message broker moves the payload to a **Dead Letter Queue (DLQ)**.
- Engineers are alerted via Application Insights / OpenTelemetry metrics (`DeadLetterMessageCount > 0`).
- The message is inspected, fixed, and re-driven through the queue without data loss.

#### C. Saga Pattern & Compensating Transactions
If a background job fails halfway through a multi-step workflow (e.g., an e-commerce order workflow where inventory was reserved, but payment capture failed), the system cannot leave resources in an orphaned state.
- An **Orchestrated Saga** (via MassTransit State Machine or Temporal) detects the failure step and dispatches **Compensating Commands** (`ReleaseInventoryReservationCommand`) to roll back previously executed operations and preserve eventual consistency.

---

### Notes & Terminology
- **ProblemDetails (RFC 7807 / RFC 9457)**: A standardized JSON format for carrying machine-readable details of errors in HTTP response payloads.
- **SignalR**: An open-source library for ASP.NET Core that enables bidirectional, real-time communication between server code and connected browser or mobile clients.
- **DLQ (Dead Letter Queue)**: A secondary sub-queue in message brokers (Azure Service Bus, RabbitMQ, AWS SQS) used to store messages that cannot be processed successfully due to poison data or system errors.
- **HMAC (Hash-based Message Authentication Code)**: A cryptographic authentication mechanism using a secret key and a hash function (e.g., SHA256) to verify data integrity and authenticity.
- **Saga Pattern**: A design pattern for managing distributed transactions across microservices through a sequence of local transactions, coordinated via events or an orchestrator, utilizing compensating actions upon failure.

### Microsoft Documentation References
- [Microsoft Learn: Handle Errors in ASP.NET Core Web APIs (ProblemDetails)](https://learn.microsoft.com/aspnet/core/web-api/handle-errors)
- [Microsoft Learn: Overview of ASP.NET Core SignalR](https://learn.microsoft.com/aspnet/core/signalr/introduction)
- [Microsoft Learn: Compensating Transaction Pattern](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/architecture/patterns/compensating-transaction)

## Question 6: Can you give an example of when to use 202 Accepted?

### Answer

### Enterprise Use Case: Bulk Catalog CSV Ingestion & Validation

A premier enterprise scenario for `202 Accepted` is **Bulk Data Import** (e.g., uploading an inventory file with 50,000 product rows in an e-commerce platform).

Processing 50,000 records requires reading file streams, schema validation, foreign key checks against the database, computing pricing discounts, and performing batch database inserts. This operation typically takes 45 to 120 seconds.

If implemented synchronously with a standard `200 OK` or `201 Created`:
1. The client connection remains open for minutes, causing **HTTP 504 Gateway Timeouts** at reverse proxies (Cloudflare, Azure Front Door, NGINX).
2. Server threads and database connections are locked up, crippling API concurrency.
3. If the connection drops during transmission, the client cannot verify if the import completed or failed.

---

### Step-by-Step Architectural Protocol Flow

```
Client                             API Gateway / Kestrel               Background Worker / Queue
  │                                          │                                     │
  │─── 1. POST /inventory/bulk-imports ────>│                                     │
  │    (multipart file + Idempotency-Key)    │                                     │
  │                                          │─── Stream file to Blob Storage ────>│
  │                                          │─── Enqueue Ingestion Message ──────>│
  │<── 2. 202 Accepted ─────────────────────│                                     │
  │    Location: /api/v1/operations/imp-8831 │                                     │
  │    Retry-After: 30                       │                                     │
  │                                          │                                     │
  │ [Client waits 30 seconds]                │                                     │
  │                                          │                                     │
  │─── 3. GET /operations/imp-8831 ─────────>│                                     │
  │<── 4. 200 OK (Status: Processing, 40%) ─│                                     │
  │    Retry-After: 15                       │                                     │
  │                                          │                                     │
  │ [Client waits 15 seconds]                │                                     │
  │                                          │                                     │
  │─── 5. GET /operations/imp-8831 ─────────>│                                     │
  │<── 6. 303 See Other ─────────────────────│                                     │
  │    Location: /inventory/batches/imp-8831 │                                     │
  │                                          │                                     │
  │─── 7. GET /inventory/batches/imp-8831 ──>│                                     │
  │<── 8. 200 OK (Summary Report) ───────────│                                     │
```

---

### Concrete Implementation in ASP.NET Core

#### 1. Ingestion Endpoint Returning `202 Accepted`
```csharp
using System.Threading.Channels;
using Microsoft.AspNetCore.Mvc;

public sealed record BulkImportCommand(Guid OperationId, Stream FileStream, string CustomerId);
public sealed record OperationStatusDto(Guid OperationId, string Status, int PercentComplete, string? ResourceUrl);

[ApiController]
[Route("api/v1/inventory/bulk-imports")]
public sealed class BulkImportController : ControllerBase
{
    private readonly ChannelWriter<BulkImportCommand> _channelWriter;
    private readonly IOperationStateRepository _stateRepository;

    public BulkImportController(
        ChannelWriter<BulkImportCommand> channelWriter, 
        IOperationStateRepository stateRepository)
    {
        _channelWriter = channelWriter;
        _stateRepository = stateRepository;
    }

    [HttpPost]
    [RequestSizeLimit(52_428_800)] // Limit upload to 50 MB
    public async Task<IActionResult> UploadCatalog(
        [FromForm] IFormFile file, 
        [FromHeader(Name = "Idempotency-Key")] string? idempotencyKey,
        CancellationToken ct)
    {
        if (file is null || file.Length == 0)
            return BadRequest("A non-empty CSV file must be provided.");

        var operationId = Guid.NewGuid();

        // 1. Initialize Operation Tracking Record in Database / Redis
        await _stateRepository.CreateOperationAsync(
            operationId, 
            status: "Accepted", 
            estimatedDurationSeconds: 45, 
            ct);

        // 2. Dispatch to an asynchronous reliable Channel or Message Broker
        var stream = file.OpenReadStream();
        await _channelWriter.WriteAsync(new BulkImportCommand(operationId, stream, "tenant-1"), ct);

        // 3. Emit 202 Accepted with Location and Retry-After headers
        Response.Headers.RetryAfter = "30";

        var statusDto = new
        {
            OperationId = operationId,
            Status = "Accepted",
            Message = "File uploaded successfully. Bulk ingestion is queued.",
            CheckStatusUrl = Url.Action(nameof(GetOperationStatus), new { id = operationId })
        };

        return AcceptedAtAction(nameof(GetOperationStatus), new { id = operationId }, statusDto);
    }

    [HttpGet("operations/{id:guid}")]
    public async Task<IActionResult> GetOperationStatus(Guid id, CancellationToken ct)
    {
        var operation = await _stateRepository.GetOperationAsync(id, ct);
        if (operation is null)
            return NotFound();

        if (operation.Status is "Processing" or "Accepted")
        {
            Response.Headers.RetryAfter = "15";
            return Ok(operation);
        }

        if (operation.Status is "Succeeded")
        {
            // Point the client to the materialized batch summary resource via 303 See Other
            return SeeOther($"/api/v1/inventory/batches/{id}/summary");
        }

        // Failed state
        return Ok(operation); // Returns ProblemDetails within the operation envelope
    }
}
```

#### 2. Background Processing Worker (`BackgroundService`)
```csharp
public sealed class CatalogIngestionWorker : BackgroundService
{
    private readonly ChannelReader<BulkImportCommand> _channelReader;
    private readonly IServiceScopeFactory _scopeFactory;

    public CatalogIngestionWorker(
        ChannelReader<BulkImportCommand> channelReader, 
        IServiceScopeFactory scopeFactory)
    {
        _channelReader = channelReader;
        _scopeFactory = scopeFactory;
    }

    protected override async Task ExecuteAsync(CancellationToken stoppingToken)
    {
        await foreach (var command in _channelReader.ReadAllAsync(stoppingToken))
        {
            using var scope = _scopeFactory.CreateScope();
            var stateRepo = scope.ServiceProvider.GetRequiredService<IOperationStateRepository>();

            try
            {
                await stateRepo.UpdateStatusAsync(command.OperationId, "Processing", percent: 10, stoppingToken);

                // Simulate batch chunk processing and database operations
                await Task.Delay(5000, stoppingToken);
                await stateRepo.UpdateStatusAsync(command.OperationId, "Processing", percent: 60, stoppingToken);

                await Task.Delay(5000, stoppingToken);
                await stateRepo.SetCompletedAsync(
                    command.OperationId, 
                    resourcePath: $"/api/v1/inventory/batches/{command.OperationId}", 
                    stoppingToken);
            }
            catch (Exception ex)
            {
                await stateRepo.SetFailedAsync(
                    command.OperationId, 
                    errorCode: "IngestionError", 
                    errorMessage: ex.Message, 
                    stoppingToken);
            }
        }
    }
}
```

---

### Notes & Terminology
- **202 Accepted**: An HTTP response status code indicating that the server has accepted the request for execution, but processing has not yet completed.
- **303 See Other**: An HTTP response status code commonly used after asynchronous processing or POST redirects to direct the client to retrieve the resulting resource using a GET method.
- **Retry-After Header**: An HTTP response header specifying the duration in seconds (or an HTTP date) that the client should wait before making a follow-up request.
- **System.Threading.Channels**: A high-performance, low-allocation in-process communication library in .NET providing thread-safe message passing between producers and consumers.
- **Multipart/form-data**: An HTTP MIME multipart encoding format used to transmit binary file uploads and structured forms across HTTP POST requests.

### Microsoft Documentation References
- [Microsoft Learn: Asynchronous Request-Reply Pattern](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/architecture/patterns/async-request-reply)
- [Microsoft Learn: System.Threading.Channels in .NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/dotnet/core/extensions/channels)
- [Microsoft Learn: File Uploads in ASP.NET Core](https://learn.microsoft.com/aspnet/core/mvc/models/file-uploads)

## Question 7: A Client Sends a PATCH Request to Update Only the Email Field. How Do You Implement Partial Updates?

### The Partial Update Dilemma

Handling partial updates in a Web API sounds simple on the surface, but in practice, it is one of the easiest ways to accidentally corrupt data.

The classic bug happens when an endpoint accepts a regular DTO: the client sends only `{"email": "new@acme.com"}`, and because the remaining DTO properties default to `null`, the server unknowingly overwrites the customer's name, address, and phone number with nulls.

To implement partial updates safely and efficiently, you need to solve three practical challenges:
1. **Choosing the Right Protocol:** Picking between the verbosity of RFC 6902 (JSON Patch) and the clean simplicity of RFC 7396 (JSON Merge Patch).
2. **Solving the Tri-State Problem:** Telling the difference between *"the client explicitly sent null to clear this field"* and *"the client omitted the field because they didn't want to change it"*.
3. **Targeted EF Core Updates:** Updating only the modified columns in SQL without loading or overwriting the entire entity graph.

---

### 1. Protocol Strategy: RFC 6902 vs. RFC 7396

| Dimension | RFC 6902: JSON Patch (`application/json-patch+json`) | RFC 7396: JSON Merge Patch (`application/merge-patch+json`) |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Payload Structure** | Array of operational instructions: `[{"op": "replace", "path": "/email", "value": "new@example.com"}]` | Partial resource representation: `{"email": "new@example.com"}` |
| **Client Developer Experience** | Cumbersome. Frontend clients must construct abstract path/operation trees. | Intuitive. Frontend simply sends an object containing only changed fields. |
| **.NET Runtime Support** | Supported via `Microsoft.AspNetCore.JsonPatch`, but traditionally coupled to `Newtonsoft.Json`. | Implemented cleanly in modern .NET via `System.Text.Json` or strongly-typed patch wrappers. |
| **Expressiveness** | Rich: supports `add`, `remove`, `replace`, `move`, `copy`, and `test` predicates. | Limited to merging: set values, reset via `null`, omit to ignore. |
| **Architectural Verdict** | Ideal for complex document manipulations (e.g., CMS trees, array index reordering). | **Preferred enterprise standard for business entities and partial DTO updates.** |

---

### 2. Solving the Tri-State Problem in Modern .NET (`Optional<T>`)

In C#, a standard nullable reference type (`string? Email`) has only two states: a value or `null`. It cannot natively express whether `email` was missing from the client's JSON payload or intentionally set to `null` to erase the record.

Inspecting raw `HttpContext.Request` or manually parsing strings is an unmaintainable anti-pattern. The enterprise solution is a generic **`Optional<T>`** (or `PatchField<T>`) wrapper with a custom `JsonConverter`:

```csharp
using System.Text.Json;
using System.Text.Json.Serialization;

[JsonConverter(typeof(OptionalJsonConverterFactory))]
public readonly struct Optional<T>
{
    public bool IsSpecified { get; }
    public T? Value { get; }

    public Optional(T? value)
    {
        Value = value;
        IsSpecified = true;
    }

    public static Optional<T> Unspecified => default;
    public static implicit operator Optional<T>(T? value) => new(value);
}

// Custom Converter Factory to detect presence during JSON deserialization
public sealed class OptionalJsonConverterFactory : JsonConverterFactory
{
    public override bool CanConvert(Type typeToConvert) =>
        typeToConvert.IsGenericType && typeToConvert.GetGenericTypeDefinition() == typeof(Optional<>);

    public override JsonConverter CreateConverter(Type typeToConvert, JsonSerializerOptions options)
    {
        var valueType = typeToConvert.GetGenericArguments()[0];
        var converterType = typeof(OptionalJsonConverter<>).MakeGenericType(valueType);
        return (JsonConverter)Activator.CreateInstance(converterType)!;
    }
}

public sealed class OptionalJsonConverter<T> : JsonConverter<Optional<T>>
{
    public override Optional<T> Read(ref Utf8JsonReader reader, Type typeToConvert, JsonSerializerOptions options)
    {
        if (reader.TokenType == JsonTokenType.Null)
            return new Optional<T>(default);

        var value = JsonSerializer.Deserialize<T>(ref reader, options);
        return new Optional<T>(value);
    }

    public override void Write(Utf8JsonWriter writer, Optional<T> value, JsonSerializerOptions options)
    {
        if (value.IsSpecified)
            JsonSerializer.Serialize(writer, value.Value, options);
    }
}
```

Now define the PATCH DTO:
```csharp
public sealed class PatchCustomerDto
{
    public Optional<string?> Email { get; init; }
    public Optional<string?> PhoneNumber { get; init; }
}
```
- If `{ "email": "alice@example.com" }` is sent: `Email.IsSpecified == true`, `Email.Value == "alice@example.com"`.
- If `{ "phoneNumber": null }` is sent: `PhoneNumber.IsSpecified == true`, `PhoneNumber.Value == null` (triggering field deletion/clearing).
- If `phoneNumber` is omitted: `PhoneNumber.IsSpecified == false` (no update applied).

---

### 3. Highly Performant Persistence in Entity Framework Core

#### Approach A: Direct SQL `ExecuteUpdateAsync` (EF Core 7+)
For high-throughput APIs where loading the entity into memory is wasteful, use `ExecuteUpdateAsync`. This compiles directly into a single SQL `UPDATE` statement without loading the record into the EF Core change tracker:

```csharp
[HttpPatch("api/v1/customers/{id:guid}")]
public async Task<IActionResult> PatchCustomer(
    Guid id, 
    [FromBody] PatchCustomerDto patchDto, 
    AppDbContext dbContext, 
    CancellationToken ct)
{
    if (!patchDto.Email.IsSpecified && !patchDto.PhoneNumber.IsSpecified)
        return BadRequest("No valid fields provided for update.");

    // Validate email format if provided
    if (patchDto.Email.IsSpecified && !IsValidEmail(patchDto.Email.Value))
        return BadRequest("Invalid email address format.");

    var query = dbContext.Customers.Where(c => c.Id == id);

    var rowsAffected = await query.ExecuteUpdateAsync(setter =>
    {
        if (patchDto.Email.IsSpecified)
            setter.SetProperty(c => c.Email, patchDto.Email.Value);

        if (patchDto.PhoneNumber.IsSpecified)
            setter.SetProperty(c => c.PhoneNumber, patchDto.PhoneNumber.Value);

        setter.SetProperty(c => c.UpdatedAtUtc, DateTime.UtcNow);
    }, ct);

    return rowsAffected > 0 ? NoContent() : NotFound();
}
```

#### Approach B: Tracking Property Modification via `Entry.Property().IsModified`
When business logic or domain events require loading the entity aggregate into memory:

```csharp
var customer = await dbContext.Customers.FirstOrDefaultAsync(c => c.Id == id, ct);
if (customer is null) return NotFound();

// Conditionally mutate and mark modified
if (patchDto.Email.IsSpecified)
{
    customer.UpdateEmail(patchDto.Email.Value); // Encapsulated Domain Method
    dbContext.Entry(customer).Property(c => c.Email).IsModified = true;
}

await dbContext.SaveChangesAsync(ct);
return NoContent();
```

---

### 4. Optimistic Concurrency Control (`If-Match` & `ETag`)
To protect against the **Lost Update Problem** (where two concurrent clients submit conflicting PATCH updates), always require the client to supply an `If-Match` header containing the resource's current `ETag` (row version or timestamp hash):

```csharp
[HttpPatch("api/v1/customers/{id:guid}")]
public async Task<IActionResult> PatchCustomer(
    Guid id,
    [FromBody] PatchCustomerDto dto,
    [FromHeader(Name = "If-Match")] string? ifMatch,
    AppDbContext dbContext,
    CancellationToken ct)
{
    if (string.IsNullOrWhiteSpace(ifMatch))
        return StatusCode(StatusCodes.Status428PreconditionRequired, "An If-Match header containing the current ETag is required.");

    var customer = await dbContext.Customers.FirstOrDefaultAsync(c => c.Id == id, ct);
    if (customer is null) return NotFound();

    var currentEtag = $"\"{Convert.ToBase64String(customer.RowVersion)}\"";
    if (ifMatch != currentEtag && ifMatch != "*")
        return StatusCode(StatusCodes.Status412PreconditionFailed, "Resource has been modified by another process.");

    // Apply updates...
    await dbContext.SaveChangesAsync(ct);
    return NoContent();
}
```

---

### Notes & Terminology
- **PATCH**: An HTTP method defined in RFC 5789 intended for applying partial modifications to an existing resource.
- **PUT**: An HTTP method defined in RFC 9110 representing complete resource replacement or idempotent creation.
- **JSON Merge Patch (RFC 7396)**: An IETF standard defining a JSON format and processing rules for describing partial changes to a target JSON document.
- **JSON Patch (RFC 6902)**: An IETF standard defining a JSON document structure for expressing a sequence of operations to apply to a target JSON document.
- **Lost Update Problem**: A concurrency failure occurring when two transactions read the same resource and subsequently update it based on the initially read values, causing the later update to overwrite the earlier update without awareness.
- **ETag (Entity Tag)**: An HTTP response header providing an opaque validator/version identifier for a specific version of a resource, used in conditional requests (`If-Match`, `If-None-Match`).

### Microsoft Documentation References
- [Microsoft Learn: JSON Patch in ASP.NET Core web API](https://learn.microsoft.com/aspnet/core/web-api/jsonpatch)
- [Microsoft Learn: ExecuteUpdate and ExecuteDelete in EF Core](https://learn.microsoft.com/ef/core/saving/execute-insert-update-delete)
- [Microsoft Learn: Handling Concurrency Conflicts in EF Core](https://learn.microsoft.com/ef/core/saving/concurrency)

---

## Question 8: When would you definitely use PATCH instead of PUT?

### Answer

### Selection Matrix

While `PUT` is simple and predictable, its requirement for **complete resource replacement** breaks down in modern distributed, high-concurrency systems. Solution architects recommend `PATCH` over `PUT` in five explicit enterprise scenarios:

---

### 1. Large Complex Aggregates with Isolated Field Mutations
When a resource aggregate contains dozens of attributes, nested collections, and binary metadata (e.g., an Enterprise `UserProfile` containing personal info, billing preferences, privacy toggles, and notification settings):
- **PUT Failure**: Forcing a client to transmit the entire 50 KB JSON payload simply to toggle `IsTwoFactorEnabled: true` creates severe network bandwidth waste and serialization overhead.
- **PATCH Superiority**: Transmitting `{"isTwoFactorEnabled": true}` consumes negligible bandwidth and isolates the operation strictly to the affected field.

---

### 2. Eliminating the Lost Update Problem Across Independent Collaborators
In collaborative applications where different users or microservices concurrently update different parts of the same entity:
- **Scenario**:
  - Service A updates `Customer.RiskScore` to `High`.
  - Concurrently, Service B updates `Customer.ShippingAddress`.
- **PUT Collision**:
  - Service A fetches the record and PUTs the entire object with the new `RiskScore`.
  - Service B, unaware of Service A's in-flight transaction, fetches the original record and PUTs its new `ShippingAddress`. Service B's PUT overwrites Service A's `RiskScore`, reverting it back to `Low`.
- **PATCH Resolution**:
  - Service A sends `PATCH { "riskScore": "High" }`.
  - Service B sends `PATCH { "shippingAddress": { ... } }`.
  - Both atomic operations succeed without overwriting each other's data.

---

### 3. Bandwidth-Constrained Environments (Mobile & IoT)
For mobile devices on cellular networks or battery-powered IoT devices operating over high-latency, metering-billed connections (e.g., satellite links, LTE-M, NB-IoT):
- Minimizing upstream transmission payload size directly correlates with battery preservation, lower latency, and reduced data transfer costs.

---

### 4. Asymmetrical Field Visibility & Role-Based Access Control (RBAC)
When certain fields of an entity are read-only for specific client roles:
- **Scenario**: A `Member` user can update their `Bio` and `DisplayName`, but only an `Administrator` can modify `Role`, `SubscriptionTier`, or `AccountStatus`.
- **PUT Dilemma**: If the Member submits a `PUT`, what happens to `Role`? If the client omits it, does the server set it to default (`Member`) or reject the request? If the client sends `Role: "Admin"`, the server must write complex over-posting sanitization logic to strip unauthorized fields.
- **PATCH Resolution**: The Member sends `PATCH { "displayName": "NewName" }`. The server validates authorization solely against the properties explicitly present in the patch payload.

---

### 5. Event-Driven Auditing & Intent Capture
In CQRS and Event Sourcing architectures, a full PUT (`ReplaceCustomer`) obscures the user's business intent:
- A `PATCH { "status": "Suspended", "reason": "NonPayment" }` directly maps to a distinct domain event: `CustomerSuspendedEvent`.
- A full `PUT` forces the domain model to perform complex diffing across all 30 fields to infer what actually changed.

---

### Comparison Summary Table

| Architectural Criteria | PUT | PATCH |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **HTTP Semantics** | Full Resource Replacement (RFC 9110) | Partial Resource Modification (RFC 5789) |
| **Idempotency** | **Naturally Idempotent** ($f(f(x)) = f(x)$) | Non-idempotent by specification (though Merge Patch is effectively idempotent) |
| **Bandwidth Efficiency** | Low (Full payload required) | **High** (Delta payload only) |
| **Concurrent Property Edits** | Prone to overwriting sibling properties | **Safe** when modifying isolated properties |
| **Over-Posting Risk** | High (Mass-assignment vulnerabilities) | Low (Only incoming keys are evaluated) |

---

### Notes & Terminology
- **Idempotency**: An API property where making multiple identical requests has the same outcome on server state as making a single request.
- **RBAC (Role-Based Access Control)**: A security access control mechanism that restricts network or resource access based on the roles of individual authorized users.
- **Over-Posting (Mass Assignment)**: A security vulnerability occurring when an API binds client-provided input directly to internal data models, allowing attackers to overwrite sensitive, unintended fields (e.g., `IsAdmin = true`).
- **IoT (Internet of Things)**: A network of physical devices equipped with sensors and software communicating over the internet.

### Microsoft Documentation References
- [Microsoft Learn: Prevent Overposting in ASP.NET Core](https://learn.microsoft.com/aspnet/core/tutorials/first-web-api#prevent-overposting)
- [Microsoft Learn: REST API Design Best Practices](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/architecture/best-practices/api-design)

---

## Question 9: How do you handle validation with partial updates?

### Answer

### Invariant vs. Delta Validation

Validation in a partial update is significantly more complex than in a `PUT` or `POST` request:
1. In a `POST` or `PUT`, all required fields must be present and valid simultaneously.
2. In a `PATCH`, fields are omitted by design. If a model validator marks `Email` as `[Required]`, an unprovided `Email` in a PATCH request will incorrectly cause validation to fail.

To resolve this, enterprise .NET architecture separates validation into two distinct phases:
- **Phase 1: Format & Syntactic Delta Validation** (Validating only the fields present in the patch payload).
- **Phase 2: Semantic Domain & Aggregate Invariant Validation** (Validating the merged state against business rules).

---

### Implementation Architecture using FluentValidation & EF Core

#### Step 1: Syntactic Validation of Provided Fields
Using **FluentValidation**, write conditional rules using `.When()` to inspect whether the field was actually specified in the incoming DTO:

```csharp
using FluentValidation;

public sealed class PatchCustomerDtoValidator : AbstractValidator<PatchCustomerDto>
{
    public PatchCustomerDtoValidator()
    {
        // Only validate Email if the client specified it in the request
        When(x => x.Email.IsSpecified, () =>
        {
            RuleFor(x => x.Email.Value)
                .NotEmpty()
                .WithMessage("Email cannot be cleared or set to an empty string.")
                .EmailAddress()
                .WithMessage("A valid email address format is required.")
                .MaximumLength(256)
                .WithMessage("Email must not exceed 256 characters.");
        });

        // Only validate PhoneNumber if the client specified it
        When(x => x.PhoneNumber.IsSpecified && x.PhoneNumber.Value is not null, () =>
        {
            RuleFor(x => x.PhoneNumber.Value)
                .Matches(@"^\+?[1-9]\d{1,14}$")
                .WithMessage("Phone number must adhere to E.164 international formatting.");
        });
    }
}
```

#### Step 2: Semantic & Aggregate Invariant Validation
Once individual fields pass syntactic checks, load the existing entity from the database, project the patch modifications into memory, and validate the **resulting merged aggregate**:

```csharp
[HttpPatch("api/v1/customers/{id:guid}")]
public async Task<IActionResult> PatchCustomer(
    Guid id,
    [FromBody] PatchCustomerDto patchDto,
    IValidator<PatchCustomerDto> validator,
    AppDbContext dbContext,
    CancellationToken ct)
{
    // Phase 1: Syntactic Delta Validation
    var validationResult = await validator.ValidateAsync(patchDto, ct);
    if (!validationResult.IsValid)
    {
        return Results.ValidationProblem(validationResult.ToDictionary());
    }

    // Load existing aggregate
    var customer = await dbContext.Customers.FirstOrDefaultAsync(c => c.Id == id, ct);
    if (customer is null) return NotFound();

    // Phase 2: Domain Invariant Check (e.g., Uniqueness constraint)
    if (patchDto.Email.IsSpecified && patchDto.Email.Value != customer.Email)
    {
        var emailInUse = await dbContext.Customers
            .AnyAsync(c => c.Email == patchDto.Email.Value && c.Id != id, ct);

        if (emailInUse)
        {
            return Problem(
                statusCode: StatusCodes.Status409Conflict,
                title: "Email Conflict",
                detail: "The specified email address is already associated with another active customer account.");
        }

        // Apply domain mutation
        customer.ChangeEmail(patchDto.Email.Value!);
    }

    if (patchDto.PhoneNumber.IsSpecified)
    {
        customer.UpdatePhoneNumber(patchDto.PhoneNumber.Value);
    }

    // Save and commit
    await dbContext.SaveChangesAsync(ct);
    return NoContent();
}
```

---

### Handling Cross-Field Dependencies in Partial Updates
Consider an entity with cross-field rules:
$$\text{If } \text{IsTaxExempt} == \text{true}, \text{ then } \text{TaxExemptionCertificateNumber} \neq \text{null}$$

If a client sends `PATCH { "isTaxExempt": true }`, the incoming DTO does not contain `TaxExemptionCertificateNumber`. 
- **Rule**: Cross-field invariants **cannot be validated against the incoming DTO alone**. They must be validated against the **merged state** of the entity before calling `SaveChangesAsync()`.
- Encapsulate this logic inside the Domain Entity:
  ```csharp
  public void EnableTaxExemption(string? certificateNumber)
  {
      if (string.IsNullOrWhiteSpace(certificateNumber) && string.IsNullOrWhiteSpace(this.TaxExemptionCertificateNumber))
      {
          throw new DomainException("Cannot mark customer as tax-exempt without an active certificate number.");
      }
      this.IsTaxExempt = true;
  }
  ```

---

### Notes & Terminology
- **FluentValidation**: A popular .NET library for building strongly-typed validation rules using a fluent interface and lambda expressions.
- **Syntactic Validation**: Validation verifying that input data adheres to structural rules, length constraints, and formats (e.g., valid email pattern, non-empty string).
- **Semantic Validation**: Validation verifying that input data satisfies business context and relational rules (e.g., verifying whether an email address is already taken in the database).
- **Domain Invariant**: A business condition or business rule in Domain-Driven Design (DDD) that must remain true at all times throughout the aggregate's lifecycle.

### Microsoft Documentation References
- [Microsoft Learn: Model Validation in ASP.NET Core](https://learn.microsoft.com/aspnet/core/mvc/models/validation)
- [Microsoft Learn: Design a DDD Microservice Domain Layer](https://learn.microsoft.com/dotnet/architecture/microservices/microservice-ddd-cqrs-patterns/net-core-microservice-domain-model)

## Question 10: Should the client send null to clear a value, or omit the field entirely? Is there a standard?

### Answer

### The IETF Standards

Yes, there is an explicit, formal internet standard governing this behavior: **RFC 7396 (JSON Merge Patch)**.

RFC 7396 establishes precise rules to eliminate ambiguity between clearing a field and leaving a field untouched:

1. **`null` Explicitly Clears the Value**:
   When a field is present in the JSON body with a value of `null`, the server must set the corresponding target resource attribute to `null` (or its default empty/unset state).
   ```json
   {
     "phoneNumber": null
   }
   ```
   *Result*: `customer.PhoneNumber` is cleared / set to `null` in the database.

2. **Omission Preserves the Value**:
   When a field is omitted entirely from the JSON payload, the server must leave the target resource attribute untouched.
   ```json
   {
     "email": "newemail@example.com"
   }
   ```
   *Result*: `customer.Email` is updated to `"newemail@example.com"`, while `customer.PhoneNumber` retains whatever value was previously persisted.

3. **Explicit Non-Null Values Update the Target**:
   ```json
   {
     "phoneNumber": "+14155552671"
   }
   ```
   *Result*: `customer.PhoneNumber` is replaced with `"+14155552671"`.

---

### The RFC 6902 (JSON Patch) Alternative Standard

If using the alternative standard, **RFC 6902 (JSON Patch)**, operations are expressed explicitly via discrete verbs:
- To clear/delete an attribute, the client sends an explicit `"remove"` operation:
  ```json
  [
    { "op": "remove", "path": "/phoneNumber" }
  ]
  ```
- To update an attribute, the client sends a `"replace"` operation:
  ```json
  [
    { "op": "replace", "path": "/email", "value": "newemail@example.com" }
  ]
  ```

---

### Why Standard Nullable Types Fail Without Architectural Wrappers

In default C# / .NET deserialization:
```csharp
public class UpdateRequest
{
    public string? Email { get; set; }
    public string? PhoneNumber { get; set; }
}
```
If the client submits `{ "email": "alice@example.com" }`:
- `request.Email == "alice@example.com"`
- `request.PhoneNumber == null`

Because the C# runtime sets unprovided reference properties to their default value (`null`), the application cannot determine whether the client sent `"phoneNumber": null` or omitted it entirely! 

If the application naively copies all non-null properties or assigns `customer.PhoneNumber = request.PhoneNumber`, it will either:
- Never allow a user to delete their phone number (if ignoring `null`).
- Accidental erase the phone number every time the user updates their email (if copying `null`).

This proves why the **`Optional<T>` / `PatchField<T>`** pattern detailed in Question 7 is a strict architectural requirement for RFC 7396 compliance in .NET.

---

### Notes & Terminology
- **RFC 7396 (JSON Merge Patch)**: The definitive IETF standard defining the merge semantics where `null` implies deletion and omission implies retention.
- **RFC 6902 (JSON Patch)**: The IETF standard defining operation-based patch instructions (`add`, `remove`, `replace`).
- **Tri-State Logic**: A programming logic condition supporting three values: Present-with-Value, Present-as-Null, and Absent/Undefined.

### Microsoft Documentation References
- [IETF RFC 7396: JSON Merge Patch](https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc7396)
- [Microsoft Learn: JSON Patch in ASP.NET Core web API](https://learn.microsoft.com/aspnet/core/web-api/jsonpatch)

---

## Question 11: What is a DTO and a nullable DTO?

### Answer

### DTO Fundamentals & Evolution

A **DTO (Data Transfer Object)** is an architectural design pattern described by Martin Fowler. It is a plain object whose sole purpose is to encapsulate data and transport it between processes, network boundaries, or architecture layers (e.g., from the API Controller to the client, or from the Application Service to the Presentation Layer).

A DTO contains **no business logic, behavior, or domain invariants**; it contains only getters, setters, or immutable properties, and serialization metadata.

---

### 1. Architectural Purpose of DTOs in Enterprise .NET

Exposing Entity Framework Core domain entities (e.g., `public class Customer`) directly from API endpoints is a severe enterprise anti-pattern:

```
[Client] <======== DTO Network Contract ========> [API Controller]
                                                         │
                                               AutoMapper / Mapster
                                                         │
                                                         ▼
[Database] <====== Entity Framework Core ======> [Domain Entity]
```

1. **Security & Mass-Assignment (Over-Posting)**:
   If an internal `User` entity has properties `Id`, `Email`, `PasswordHash`, and `IsAdmin`, binding an incoming HTTP payload directly to the `User` entity allows an attacker to send `{ "isAdmin": true }` and escalate privileges. A DTO (e.g., `RegisterUserRequestDto`) exposes only `Email` and `Password`.
2. **Decoupling Database Schema from Network Contracts**:
   Renaming a database column or splitting a database table must never break external API consumers. DTOs establish an immutable, versioned public contract independent of relational table structures.
3. **Preventing Serialization Cycles**:
   Entity models with bi-directional navigation properties (`Order` -> `OrderItems` -> `Order`) trigger fatal circular reference exceptions during JSON serialization (`JsonException: A possible object cycle was detected`). DTOs flatten data into cleanly serializable tree structures.
4. **Performance & Bandwidth Optimization**:
   DTOs allow projecting only the required columns from the database via EF Core LINQ `.Select(x => new ProductSummaryDto(...))`, eliminating the overhead of retrieving unneeded columns into memory.

---

### 2. What is a Nullable DTO?

A **Nullable DTO** refers to a Data Transfer Object where properties are explicitly declared as nullable (using C# nullable reference and value types, such as `string?`, `int?`, `DateTime?`, or `Optional<T>`).

#### Example in Modern C#:
```csharp
public sealed record UpdateEmployeeDto(
    string? FirstName,
    string? LastName,
    DateTime? DateOfBirth,
    decimal? Salary
);
```

#### Primary Architectural Use Cases:
1. **Representing Optional Input Data (Search/Filtering Queries)**:
   In query string contracts where consumers filter by arbitrary criteria (`GET /api/v1/products?category=tech&minPrice=100`):
   ```csharp
   public sealed record ProductFilterDto(
       string? Category, 
       decimal? MinPrice, 
       decimal? MaxPrice, 
       bool? InStockOnly);
   ```
   If a parameter is omitted from the query string, it cleanly evaluates to `null`, allowing dynamic LINQ queries:
   ```csharp
   if (filter.MinPrice.HasValue)
       query = query.Where(p => p.Price >= filter.MinPrice.Value);
   ```
2. **Representing Nullable Domain Attributes**:
   Attributes that are legitimately optional in business domains (e.g., `AddressLine2?`, `MiddleName?`, `TaxExemptionCode?`).
3. **Partial Updates (PATCH) with Caution**:
   As established in Question 10, relying on a naive nullable DTO for partial updates creates ambiguity between "field was omitted" and "field was explicitly set to null". Therefore, for PATCH operations, nullable DTO properties should either be paired with an `Optional<T>` wrapper or used in PUT scenarios where null explicitly overwrites the target state.

---

### Notes & Terminology
- **DTO (Data Transfer Object)**: A design pattern used to pass data between software subsystems without business logic or behaviors.
- **POCO (Plain Old CLR Object)**: A basic .NET class unencumbered by framework-specific inheritance or attributes.
- **Over-Posting (Mass Assignment)**: A security flaw where untrusted client input binds to internal fields not meant for public exposure.
- **Nullable Reference Types (NRT)**: A C# language feature introduced in C# 8.0 that provides compile-time safety and warnings against null-reference exceptions by distinguishing between `string` (non-null) and `string?` (nullable).
- **Circular Reference**: A serialization error occurring when an object graph references itself in an infinite loop.

### Microsoft Documentation References
- [Microsoft Learn: Create Data Transfer Objects (DTOs)](https://learn.microsoft.com/aspnet/web-api/overview/data/using-web-api-with-entity-framework/part-5)
- [Microsoft Learn: Nullable Reference Types in C#](https://learn.microsoft.com/dotnet/csharp/nullable-references)
- [Microsoft Learn: Prevent Overposting in ASP.NET Core](https://learn.microsoft.com/aspnet/core/tutorials/first-web-api#prevent-overposting)
